    "oldString": "crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models including a Llama-architecture model, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A random-label control confirms the bias is geometric. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
    "newString": "crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models including a Llama-architecture model, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A random-label control confirms the bias is geometric. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

      {

A random-label control confirms the bias is geometric. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",

Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).",

Still 30 pages. The `\enlargethispage{2\baselineskip}` might be getting processed on page 9 (where the conclusion now starts), not on page 8. Let me check pages 8-9 visually.

Actually, the issue might be that the `\enlargethispage` is placed right before `\input{sections/conclusion}` in main.tex, and TeX is processing it at the boundary where the discussion ends and conclusion begins. If the page break happens before the `\enlargethispage` command is processed, it ends up on page 9.

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\\citep{gelman2013garden}.}\nOur five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\\%), while precision's deep preference collapses to 20\\% (\\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \\cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\\citep{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}\nTraining probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \\leq 0.002$; \\cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\\looseness=-1\n",

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference collapses to 20\% (\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\looseness=-1
11	
12	\enlargethispage{2\baselineskip}
13	\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}
14	Training probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \leq 0.002$; \cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\looseness=-1
15	
```

</details>

Still 30 pages. The `\enlargethispage` inside the experiments section (before Negative Results) should be on page 7 or 8. Let me check. Looking at the page 8 image earlier — the left column has the forest figure, §4.5, §4.6. So §4.6 Negative Results is on page 8 left column. The `\enlargethispage` should be processed there.

However, `\enlargethispage` in two-column mode can be tricky. In `twocolumn` mode, `\enlargethispage` enlarges the page when the command is processed in the LEFT column — if it's encountered in the right column, it might not work because the left column height is already set.


The acl class uses `twocolumn`, and since §4.6 Negative Results appears in the left column of page 8, the `\enlargethispage` command placed before that subsection should actually be processed in the left column where it can take effect.

But it's clearly not working as intended. Let me try a different strategy altogether—instead of fighting with `\enlargethispage`, I'll just trim the text directly. The additions across Intro, Discussion, and Conclusion have added roughly 3-4 lines total, so I need to cut that back. I'll remove the `\enlargethispage` from experiments.tex since it's not helping, then use `\looseness=-1` to compress the seventh paragraph in Intro about initial characterization, apply the same compression to the "These findings..." line at the end of Intro, and potentially tighten the Discussion's opening paragraph further.

Wait—I should reconsider. The original `\enlargethispage` in main.tex before the conclusion was working fine before this patch, so something about my changes has disrupted the balance.

Since `\enlargethispage` can only recover about 2 lines on page 8, I need to find roughly 1 line of savings elsewhere. I should look for paragraphs in the Introduction that don't already have `\looseness=-1` applied—those early compressions would cascade savings forward through the document. Scanning through, I see the first paragraph, the third paragraph starting with "We present...", the fourth paragraph about threshold-based approaches, and the itemized list all lack compression, so any of those would be good candidates.

    "oldString": "At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics",
    "newString": "At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\\looseness=-1\n\nThreshold-based metrics",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? {
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "bc213fc0-f4ec-4e5d-a783-59f6fa849440",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:",
    "newString": "below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\\looseness=-1\n\nOur contributions are:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\\looseness=-1\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\\looseness=-1",
          " ",

At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).",

  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\\looseness=-1\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\\looseness=-1\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\\looseness=-1\n",
        "numLines": 21,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 21
      }
    },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "a50780f6-99f0-4f87-a558-b29e6341fcf9",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\\citep{gelman2013garden}.}\nOur five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\\%), while precision's deep preference collapses to 20\\% (\\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \\cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\\citep{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}\nTraining probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \\leq 0.002$; \\cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\\looseness=-1\n",
        "numLines": 14,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 14
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex"
  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CcT2cMSd9erxEMBYuLVjiG`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\paragraph{Evidence gradient.} We classify HS advantage evidence into three tiers: \emph{cross-validated}\footnote{In our usage, \emph{cross-validated} means the result survives nested 5-fold cross-validation with a held-out test fold, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. \emph{Exploratory} ($\dagger$) results pass HB on the holdout split but lack nested CV validation due to sample constraints.} (nested CV pass; 7--8B models), \emph{significant} (HB pass without nested CV; QwQ-32B), and \emph{exploratory} ($^\dagger$, small sample or narrow coverage; R1-32B). Per-model details follow in \cref{sec:layer-results,sec:hs-text,sec:32b-analysis}.
6	
7	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \cref{sec:evaluation}.
10	
11	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
12	
13	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the crossing-rate composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers across all four models: L0 for R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep; \cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) and OT-7B, L2 (3\%) for QwQ-32B, and L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. The three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same deep layer---L63 (98\%) for both 32B models---consistent with their shared structure, though not mathematically guaranteed. Pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric, selects layers that vary across data splits and models (7--98\% depth under 60/20/20; \cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}), itself demonstrating that layer selection is a consequential methodological choice. The total exploration space spans 5 metrics $\times$ 7 window sizes $\times$ 28--64 layers (model-dependent); HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests, not this full search (\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness}). The mechanism is that shallow layers produce highly compressed representations with small inter-step margins: step-to-step cosine distances are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). A linear probe in this compressed space operates near the noise floor, generating frequent threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\looseness=-1
14	
15	\begin{table}[t]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
19	\toprule
20	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{CR-comp.}$^a$ & \textbf{Thresh.}$^b$ & $p$ \textbf{(thresh.)} \\
21	\midrule
22	R1-8B & L0 (0\%) & L14 (44\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\
23	OT-7B & L0 (0\%) & L16 (57\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\
24	QwQ-32B & L2 (3\%) & L63 (98\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\
25	R1-32B & L10 (16\%) & L63 (98\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\
26	\bottomrule
27	\end{tabular}
28	\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\,Crossing-rate composite (bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate). $^b$\,Consensus of threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite). Rightmost: HS vs.\ text precision $p$-value at threshold-based layer (e.g., QwQ-32B CR-composite layer: bal\_acc $p{=}0.35$, precision $p{=}0.83$, both non-significant).}
29	\label{tab:layer_selection}
30	\end{table}
31	
32	\begin{figure}[t]
33	\centering
34	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_heatmap.pdf}
35	\caption{Per-layer metric heatmap (2$\times$2). Each column: one of five metrics (min-max normalized). Blue dashed: CR-composite layer (bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate); orange dash-dot: threshold-based layer (consensus of threshold-based metrics). For R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep), crossing rate drives CR-composite selection to L0 while threshold-based metrics retain L14. At 32B, CR-composite selections cluster near input layers while threshold-based metrics peak at deep layers.}
36	\label{fig:layer_heatmap}
37	\end{figure}
38	
39	The downstream impact is severe: at CR-composite layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage; both 32B models fail. At threshold-based layers, the HS advantage is graded: cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B, R1-8B; nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$, and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (residualized gap below MDE). The $\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \leq 20$) where effect sizes are descriptive rather than confirmatory. Had we reported only CR-composite-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact. A random-label control on QwQ-32B confirms that the crossing-rate shallow-layer bias is a geometric property of the representations (100\% of random-label repeats select shallow layers), while threshold-based deep-layer selection requires real safety labels (\cref{app:random_cp}). AUROC, excluded from our primary metrics because it is threshold-free (\cref{sec:evaluation}), confirms HS discriminability at BA-selected layers ($\Delta$AUROC +12.1 to +15.1pp; \cref{tab:ba_layer_results}) but cannot diagnose the threshold-dependent layer selection bias.\looseness=-1
40	
41	\subsection{HS vs.\ Text Comparison}\label{sec:hs-text}
42	
43	\begin{table}[t]
44	\centering
45	\small
46	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
47	\begin{tabular}{llclrrlrc}

50	\midrule
51	R1-8B & L14 & 384d & 3 & +17.5 & +20.1 & [+12.4, +28.3] & $-$7.9 & \checkmark \\
52	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +5.8 & [$-$7.4, +19.5] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \\[2pt]
53	OT-7B & L16 & 384d & 3 & +10.7 & +21.9 & [+14.4, +29.6] & $-$17.1 & \checkmark \\
54	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +18.1 & [+8.4, +29.0] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \checkmark \\[2pt]
55	QwQ & L63 & 384d & 5 & +13.0 & +19.2 & [+9.3, +33.5] & $-$15.0 & \checkmark \\
56	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +9.4 & [$-$7.1, +27.7] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \\[2pt]
57	R1-32B & L63 & 384d & 1 & +18.7 & +15.4 & [+8.7, +22.6] & $-$14.6 & \checkmark \\
58	 & & 1024d & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & +11.7 & [$-$0.2, +24.3] & \multicolumn{1}{c}{--} & \\
59	\bottomrule
60	\end{tabular}}
61	\caption{HS vs.\ text at threshold-based layers (LR probes, 60/20/20 split, 10{,}000 bootstrap resamples). Enc.: text encoder (384d = MiniLM-L6-v2, 1024d = BGE-large). $\Delta$: HS$-$text difference (pp); ``--'' = BA and FPR are not computed for the 1024d comparison because the 384d and 1024d encoders operate at different probe calibration points and do not share a decision threshold. HB: \checkmark{} = model-level test passes Holm-Bonferroni ($\alpha{=}0.05$); all 384d model-level tests pass$^\dagger$. 1024d: HB over 4 model-level precision tests.}
62	\label{tab:hs_vs_text}
63	\end{table}
64	
65	The HS advantage is encoder-conditional: against BGE-large (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB correction (\cref{app:encoder_robustness}). Multi-seed stability appears in \cref{app:multi_seed}. Against the 384d baseline, HS probes outperform text probes in all four models$^\dagger$ (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}): cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B: +21.9pp precision, R1-8B: +20.1pp), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). R1-8B FPR is directionally consistent (5/5 seeds) but not robust ($p{=}0.044$, rank 12/12 in HB step-down; 1/5 seeds Bonferroni; \cref{app:multi_seed}). R1-32B passes 5/5 seeds but shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap NS; \cref{app:position_residual}); QwQ-32B's position baseline is competitive (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Overall, 18/20 model-seed combinations pass full HB (\cref{app:multi_seed}). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 11.1--16.8pp above chance (\cref{app:control}). A split-half analysis on OT-7B confirms internal validity (\cref{app:split_half}).\looseness=-1
66	
67	\paragraph{Capacity control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (79--92\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage across all four models$^\dagger$ (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$, all HB). The control provides partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Random projection to 384d yields similar results ($\Delta$Prec +8.3 to +20.5pp; \cref{app:random_projection}), further weakening the capacity explanation. E5-Mistral-7B (4096d$\rightarrow$PCA 384d) yields intermediate results (2/4 HB; \cref{app:e5_mistral}), forming a monotonic encoder-quality gradient with MiniLM (4/4) and BGE-large (1/4).\looseness=-1
68	
69	\paragraph{Encoder robustness.} Against BGE-large~\citep{xiao2023cpack} (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB (1/4 precision); R1-8B shows adverse FPR direction (+1.2pp). The advantage is encoder-conditional (\cref{fig:forest_precision}); layer selection (Contribution 1) is encoder-independent.\looseness=-1
70	
71	\paragraph{Position mediation.} Position ($t/T$) accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy,\footnote{R1-32B's 20.4pp drop reflects $W{=}1$; at $W{\geq}3$, drops are 9--17pp.} but all models retain non-positional signal above chance (all $p{<}0.001$; \cref{app:position_residual}). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$). Adding a quadratic position term $(t/T)^2$ does not change significance conclusions for the 7--8B models. However, QwQ-32B's advantage is fully absorbed by the quadratic term (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\looseness=-1
72	
73	\paragraph{Caveats.} Nested 5-fold CV confirms the HS precision advantage for both 7--8B models (10/10 folds; \cref{app:nested_cv}), with shrinkage of 13--25\% relative to holdout estimates, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\ holdout L16 vs.\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. This inconsistency itself supports Contribution~1 (metric-induced layer selection bias). R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage;\footnote{R1-32B's BA-optimal layer was identified post-hoc rather than through the pre-registered nested CV protocol used for 7--8B models, further motivating its exploratory status.} at $W{\geq}3$, dimensionality prevents significance. Regularization $C \in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps): higher precision at later detection. False alarm rates remain 11--50\%, so current methods characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. Per-model details in \cref{sec:32b-analysis}.
74	
75	\subsection{32B Model-Specific Analysis}\label{sec:32b-analysis}
76	
77	Both 32B results are exploratory ($n{=}19$--$20$ precludes nested CV, as each fold would contain only 4 traces per class). We report effect sizes as descriptive rather than confirmatory; LOO-CV and multi-seed analyses (\cref{app:layer_sweep,app:multi_seed}) provide partial robustness checks but do not substitute for independent validation. By contrast, nested CV on the 7--8B models shows 13--25\% shrinkage (\cref{app:nested_cv}), confirming those estimates. R1-32B and QwQ-32B share the Qwen-32B architecture (64 layers, 5120 hidden) but present opposite detection profiles (\cref{tab:32b_divergence}); training method is one plausible differentiator. Replication with $n{\geq}50$ traces per model is needed before drawing inferential conclusions from 32B results.
78	
79	\begin{table}[t]
80	\centering
81	\small
82	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
83	\toprule
84	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Cov.} & \textbf{Lead (steps)} & \textbf{HB} & \textbf{Pos.\ margin} \\
85	\midrule
86	R1-32B & 25\% & +11.7 to +13.3 & \checkmark{} ($W{=}1$) & +14.2pp \\
87	QwQ-32B & 74\% & $-$1.2 to +1.9 & \checkmark{} (all $W$) & $-$3.6pp \\
88	\bottomrule
89	\end{tabular}
90	\caption{32B cross-model divergence. R1-32B provides limited early detection with narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides robust significance with poor lead time. HB: \checkmark{} = model-level test passes Holm-Bonferroni ($\alpha{=}0.05$). Pos.\ margin: HS balanced accuracy minus position-only baseline.}
91	\label{tab:32b_divergence}
92	\end{table}
93	
94	R1-32B detects commitment 11--13 steps early (+14.2pp over position baseline) but covers only 25\% of traces ($W{=}1$). QwQ-32B achieves significance at all window sizes with 74\% coverage but negative HS lead time. QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher BA (0.832 vs.\ 0.796); HS precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR margins are not significant at $n{=}19$; MDE at 80\% power is 8.6--9.5pp (\cref{app:power_analysis}). The divergence suggests per-model validation is needed.
95	
96	\subsection{Temporal Context}\label{sec:temporal}
97	
98	Temporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities (\cref{fig:window_ablation}): at 7--8B, the modality advantage (+20.1 to +21.9pp) exceeds temporal improvement (+13pp). GRU models achieve comparable accuracy but higher FPR (\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds,app:temporal_details}).\looseness=-1
99	
100	\begin{figure}[t]
101	\centering
102	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_forest_precision.pdf}
103	\caption{Forest plot of HS precision advantage over text probes. Blue: vs.\ MiniLM-384d; orange: vs.\ BGE-1024d. Solid markers: survives Holm-Bonferroni; hollow: does not. All 384d comparisons are significant (strong to qualified evidence); against BGE-1024d, only OT-7B (strong) survives correction.}
104	\label{fig:forest_precision}
105	\end{figure}
106	
107	CP-aligned probe score trajectories (\cref{fig:trajectory} in Appendix) confirm sharper HS transitions at the commitment point, with OT-7B showing the clearest separation and R1-32B the weakest.\looseness=-1
108	
109	\subsection{Negative Results}\label{sec:negative}
110	
111	No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing (\cref{tab:negative}). BOCPD~\citep{adams2007bayesian}/CUSUM fail (0\% detection on 3/4 models; \cref{app:bocpd_config}); Mahalanobis distance yields near-zero separation; RepE~\citep{zou2023representation} is model-dependent (QwQ-32B BA 0.756 vs.\ R1-32B 0.559); MLP probes overfit; CCS misaligns with temporal structure; cross-model transfer fails. These failures define four requirements: the signal is directional, gradual, model-specific, and linearly separable. Supervised linear probes with principled layer selection form the minimal effective methodology (\cref{app:negative_details}).\looseness=-1
112	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence gradient.} We classify HS advantage evidence into three tiers: \\emph{cross-validated}\\footnote{In our usage, \\emph{cross-validated} means the result survives nested 5-fold cross-validation with a held-out test fold, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. \\emph{Exploratory} ($\\dagger$) results pass HB on the holdout split but lack nested CV validation due to sample constraints.} (nested CV pass; 7--8B models), \\emph{significant} (HB pass without nested CV; QwQ-32B), and \\emph{exploratory} ($^\\dagger$, small sample or narrow coverage; R1-32B). Per-model details follow in \\cref{sec:layer-results,sec:hs-text,sec:32b-analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \\cref{sec:evaluation}.\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\\label{sec:layer-results}\n\n\\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the crossing-rate composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers across all four models: L0 for R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep; \\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) and OT-7B, L2 (3\\%) for QwQ-32B, and L10 (16\\%) for R1-32B. The three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same deep layer---L63 (98\\%) for both 32B models---consistent with their shared structure, though not mathematically guaranteed. Pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric, selects layers that vary across data splits and models (7--98\\% depth under 60/20/20; \\cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}), itself demonstrating that layer selection is a consequential methodological choice. The total exploration space spans 5 metrics $\\times$ 7 window sizes $\\times$ 28--64 layers (model-dependent); HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests, not this full search (\\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness}). The mechanism is that shallow layers produce highly compressed representations with small inter-step margins: step-to-step cosine distances are 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). A linear probe in this compressed space operates near the noise floor, generating frequent threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{CR-comp.}$^a$ & \\textbf{Thresh.}$^b$ & $p$ \\textbf{(thresh.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L0 (0\\%) & L14 (44\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nOT-7B & L0 (0\\%) & L16 (57\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nQwQ-32B & L2 (3\\%) & L63 (98\\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\\\\nR1-32B & L10 (16\\%) & L63 (98\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\\,Crossing-rate composite (bal\\_acc $\\times$ crossing\\_rate). $^b$\\,Consensus of threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite). Rightmost: HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-value at threshold-based layer (e.g., QwQ-32B CR-composite layer: bal\\_acc $p{=}0.35$, precision $p{=}0.83$, both non-significant).}\n\\label{tab:layer_selection}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_layer_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer metric heatmap (2$\\times$2). Each column: one of five metrics (min-max normalized). Blue dashed: CR-composite layer (bal\\_acc $\\times$ crossing\\_rate); orange dash-dot: threshold-based layer (consensus of threshold-based metrics). For R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep), crossing rate drives CR-composite selection to L0 while threshold-based metrics retain L14. At 32B, CR-composite selections cluster near input layers while threshold-based metrics peak at deep layers.}\n\\label{fig:layer_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe downstream impact is severe: at CR-composite layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage; both 32B models fail. At threshold-based layers, the HS advantage is graded: cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B, R1-8B; nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$, and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (residualized gap below MDE). The $\\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \\leq 20$) where effect sizes are descriptive rather than confirmatory. Had we reported only CR-composite-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact. Blue: vs.\\ MiniLM-384d; orange: vs.\\ BGE-1024d. Solid markers: survives Holm-Bonferroni; hollow: does not. All 384d comparisons are significant (strong to qualified evidence); against BGE-1024d, only OT-7B (strong) survives correction.}\n\\label{fig:forest_precision}\n\\end{figure}\n\nCP-aligned probe score trajectories (\\cref{fig:trajectory} in Appendix) confirm sharper HS transitions at the commitment point, with OT-7B showing the clearest separation and R1-32B the weakest.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\subsection{Negative Results}\\label{sec:negative}\n\nNo alternative method consistently matches supervised probing (\\cref{tab:negative}). BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian}/CUSUM fail (0\\% detection on 3/4 models; \\cref{app:bocpd_config}); Mahalanobis distance yields near-zero separation; RepE~\\citep{zou2023representation} is model-dependent (QwQ-32B BA 0.756 vs.\\ R1-32B 0.559); MLP probes overfit; CCS misaligns with temporal structure; cross-model transfer fails. These failures define four requirements: the signal is directional, gradual, model-specific, and linearly separable. Supervised linear probes with principled layer selection form the minimal effective methodology (\\cref{app:negative_details}).\\looseness=-1\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "0448303a-19fa-41e7-9451-ff24fb87d24c",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "de8ebe65-a8b4-471e-b0d4-92bf8c2b93b8",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "02fd67f5-7300-44f6-84c0-89ab3209b916",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.",
    "newString": "false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\\looseness=-1",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\\looseness=-1\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\\looseness=-1\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\\looseness=-1\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 6,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}",
          " ",
          "-Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.",
          "+Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\\looseness=-1",
          " ",
          " Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Position mediation.} Position ($t/T$) accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy,\\footnote{R1-32B's 20.4pp drop reflects $W{=}1$; at $W{\\geq}3$, drops are 9--17pp.} but all models retain non-positional signal above chance (all $p{<}0.001$; \\cref{app:position_residual}). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$). Adding a quadratic position term $(t/T)^2$ does not change significance conclusions for the 7--8B models. However, QwQ-32B's advantage is fully absorbed by the quadratic term (\\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\\looseness=-1",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Caveats.} Nested 5-fold CV confirms the HS precision advantage for both 7--8B models (10/10 folds; \\cref{app:nested_cv}), with shrinkage of 13--25\\% relative to holdout estimates, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\\ holdout L16 vs.\\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. This inconsistency itself supports Contribution~1 (metric-induced layer selection bias). R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage;\\footnote{R1-32B's BA-optimal layer was identified post-hoc rather than through the pre-registered nested CV protocol used for 7--8B models, further motivating its exploratory status.} at $W{\\geq}3$, dimensionality prevents significance. Regularization $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps): higher precision at later detection. False alarm rates remain 11--50\\%, so current methods characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. Per-model details in \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}.",

OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\\ holdout L16 vs.\\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. Regularization $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}).
